I suppose I could try to do that, Doctor. I really could. The thing is, it's just so hard to know when it's going to happen next, you see? It's not like I wake up in the morning and think, \"Oh, today's the day I'm going to get stuck holding the milk jug.\" It just sneaks up on me. Like that time at the church dinner I was telling you about, or the time I was trying to hang my good blue cardigan on the clothesline—the one with the pearl buttons my sister gave me for my sixtieth birthday—and I just froze right there with the clothespin in my hand. Mrs. Higgins was out in her yard, too, and she must have thought I was out of my mind, just standing there with my arm up in the air like I was waving to a bird or something. \n\nBut if it does happen again, and if there's someone around—which is a big \"if,\" considering I live alone and I'm only really around people on Sundays or when I go to the Piggly Wiggly on Thursday mornings—I suppose I could ask them to try and move my arm. Or, well, I guess I wouldn't be able to ask them right then and there, because I can't really talk when I'm stuck like that. I'd have to explain it to them beforehand, wouldn't I? I'd have to say, \"Now, if you see me freeze up like a statue, just come over and give my arm a little push and see what happens.\" Oh my, can you imagine the look on Pastor Thomas's face if I asked him to do that? He's such a serious man, always talking about the book of Leviticus and looking so stern. I think he'd be terribly uncomfortable. \n\nBut I understand why you're asking, Doctor. You need to know if it feels stiff or if it's... what did you say? Like a soft, flexible object. Like a ragdoll, maybe. I just don't know how I'd manage to get someone to test it for me without causing a whole commotion. People get so flustered when things aren't normal. They don't know what to do with their hands or where to look. But... I'll try to keep it in mind. If I'm with someone, maybe my friend Martha—though she's so forgetful she'd probably think I was just playing a game with her—I'll try to see if they can move me, and I'll pay real close attention to how it feels. I'll do my best to remember, I really will, but it's just such a strange thing to plan for, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you are walking or going about your day, have you noticed that your movements have become slower, or that you have developed any shaking in your hands when they are just resting at your sides?\n\nPatient: Well... slower? I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm not running any foot races these days, being seventy-one and all, but I get around just as well as I did five, maybe even ten years ago. I walk down to the mailbox every single morning right at ten o'clock when Mr. Peterson delivers the mail—he's been our mailman for... oh, it must be fifteen years now, such a nice man, always waves at me. And I still do all my own vacuuming, too. Pushing that heavy old Hoover around the living room rug is quite the chore, especially getting around the edges of the coffee table, but I get it done in the exact same amount of time I always have. I haven't slowed down a bit, really, unless you count being careful on the porch steps when they're wet, but that's just plain common sense, isn't it? You don't want to take a tumble at my age. \n\nAnd as for shaking... no, no, my hands don't shake at all. Not when I'm just sitting there, and not when I'm doing things, either. Now, my friend Martha, the one I was telling you about with all the pills? Her hands shake terribly. We were sitting out on her patio just last Sunday having a nice glass of sweet tea, and the ice cubes in her glass were just clinking and clattering the whole time because her hands tremble so much, even when they're just resting right there in her lap. It makes me feel so bad for her, it really does. She says it's just her nerves, but I don't know. \n\nBut me? No, my hands are steady as a rock. I can still thread a needle on the first try most days, well, as long as I have my good bifocals on and the light from the window is hitting it just right. I was just hemming a pair of slacks last week, the brown ones I like to wear to the grocery store because they don't show the dirt if I brush up against the car, and my hands didn't shake one bit. So when I'm just sitting in my parlor watching my afternoon programs, my hands are perfectly still. Just resting there on the armrests, quiet as can be. It's really just that strange freezing up business with my arms that's the trouble. No shaking, and no slowing down. Just steady, regular old me.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, are you still fully aware of everything happening around you, or does it feel like you've drifted off and aren't really there anymore?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I haven't drifted off anywhere. I am fully aware. Yes, indeed. I haven't gone daydreaming or fallen asleep standing up, if that's what you mean. I am right there in the room, wide awake, seeing and hearing every little thing. And I'll tell you, Doctor, that is exactly the hardest part of it all, being so aware. In fact, to be perfectly honest with you, that is the whole reason I finally made the appointment to come see you in the first place. I came because I keep doing these terribly odd things, especially over at the church, and I know I'm doing them while it's happening! \n\nIt’s just so embarrassing. Like at the fellowship hall for our Sunday potluck—we always have a big spread on the first Sunday of the month, and Mary brings her green bean casserole with those little crispy onions on top, which everyone just loves. Well, I was standing right by the potluck table, right at the front of the line where everyone is holding their paper plates and getting hungry, and I just got stuck. I was holding the big plastic serving spoon straight up in the air, and I just wasn't moving. I was frozen there like a statue with that spoon, looking down at the macaroni salad, and I could hear everyone behind me. I could hear Mr. Davis clearing his throat—he always gets so impatient when it comes to his food—and I knew they were all staring, but I just couldn't bring my arm down. \n\nAnd it’s not just the freezing up like that, either. Sometimes I get stuck doing something over and over, but I'm fully awake for that, too. Like with the napkins. Just the other week, I was supposed to be setting out the paper napkins for the dessert table—we had a lovely lemon pound cake that afternoon—and I just stood there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. I'd fold them in half, then unfold them, then fold them again, smoothing down the edges with my thumb, just doing it again and again. And the whole time, there were people waiting on me to finish so they could get their cake and their coffee! I could see them out of the corner of my eye, whispering to each other, probably wondering if poor Mildred had finally lost her marbles, but I just kept folding that same little stack. \n\nSo, you see, I haven't drifted off to some other place at all. I know exactly what's going on around me. I know I'm doing these odd things, and I know people are waiting on me or staring at me, but I just get stuck doing them anyway. It’s the strangest, most frustrating feeling in the world, knowing you're making a spectacle of yourself but not being able to just stop and act normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Doctor, I... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know the answer to that. Like I was saying a few minutes ago about trying to get someone to test it for me... nobody has ever actually come up and touched my arm when I’m stuck in one of those spells. 

People are so funny about personal space, aren't they? It's not like the old days where a neighbor would just step right in to help. When I was frozen there with that big plastic serving spoon at the church potluck, Mr. Davis was standing right behind me—close enough that I could smell the peppermint he always keeps in his shirt pocket—but did he reach out and try to pull my arm down or take the spoon from me? No, sir, he certainly did not. He just stood there clearing his throat and making a fuss. 

So I just don't have the faintest idea if my arm would stay put like one of those mannequins in the department store windows—you know, like the ones they used to have at the Sears before it closed down out on the highway, the ones with the painted-on eyelashes—or if it would just snap right back into place like an old rubber band. Since I live all by myself in that big house, just me and my knickknacks, there's nobody around to try moving me when it happens at home. And when I'm out and about, well, folks just stare. They just stare and whisper to each other behind their hands. They don't want to get involved, I suppose, or maybe they're afraid they'll hurt me. 

So unless somebody gets brave and decides to grab hold of my arm the next time I turn into a statue, I really couldn't say what would happen. I wish I could be more helpful to you, Doctor, I really do, because I want to get to the bottom of this just as much as you do, but I just haven't had anyone try it yet.
=====================================

It's nice to meet you; could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Dr. Smith. Well, it's nice to meet you too, I suppose. I... well, I came in today because things have just been getting so strange lately, mostly over at the church. I go every Sunday, have for forty years, and I always help out with the fellowship meals. I like to be helpful, you know, since I don't work and never had much schooling, it's my way of doing my part. \n\nBut lately... I just don't know what comes over me. I keep doing these odd things, just real strange things. Like, just the other week, I was standing by the potluck table, right? They had me on the hot dishes. And I picked up the serving spoon for the green beans, and I just held it right up in the air and... I just stopped. I didn't move at all. It's the strangest thing, Doctor, because if I was doing it for a reason, even a foolish reason, I think I could understand it better, but there just isn't any reason at all.\n\nDoctor: When you are stuck in those positions, like holding the spoon in the air, if I were to gently try to move your arm, would it feel like it's stuck in place, or would it move easily like a mannequin's arm?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a good question, Doctor. You know, I've thought about that myself, wondering what it would feel like if someone just reached out and tried to pull my arm down when I was holding that spoon. Because, you see, nobody actually did try to move me. They just stood there, staring. I think they were too surprised or maybe a little frightened to touch me, which I understand. So since nobody has ever actually taken hold of my arm or tried to push it down or move it out of the way, I really couldn't tell you what it would feel like for them or for me. I just know that I don't move it myself until the spell, or whatever you want to call it, finally breaks.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these physical episodes, have you ever heard voices that others couldn't hear, or seen things that weren't really there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, doctor. No, nothing like that. My goodness, if I started hearing voices on top of the spoon business, I just don't know what I'd do. I live all by myself, you see, ever since my sister passed away—oh, it's been about six years now, bless her heart. So my little house is very, very quiet. Sometimes a bit too quiet, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. The only voices I hear are when I turn on my television programs in the afternoon, or... well, sometimes if Mrs. Higgins next door is fussing at her little dog, Buster. He's a sweet little terrier mix but he just loves to dig up her petunias, so she's always hollering out the back door at him. But inside my own head? Or in an empty room? No, never. \n\nI mean, my hearing isn't quite what it used to be, what with me being seventy-one now, but I definitely don't hear people talking who aren't really there. And as for seeing things... well, my eyesight is getting a little fuzzy around the edges. I actually had to get new bifocals just last year because I couldn't even read the hymnal at church anymore without squinting terribly, and Pastor Thomas prints them in a nice big font, too. But I only see what's really right in front of me. Like the napkins, or the hot dishes. I've never seen a ghost or a vision or anything out of the ordinary like that. Just the regular old world. It's really just my body doing these strange freezing up things... my mind itself is as quiet as can be. Just me and the ticking clock in the parlor most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any other new physical health problems, or have you started taking any new medications recently?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. My health has been pretty steady, thank the Lord. I mean, I have my usual aches and pains, of course. My knees get a little stiff when it rains—we had that terrible downpour last Tuesday, and I could hardly get up the porch steps to get the mail, but that's just getting older, isn't it? Dr. Henderson, my regular doctor, he always says, \"Mildred, at seventy-one, you're doing better than most,\" which is nice of him to say, even if he does always keep me waiting in his office for nearly an hour. \n\nBut as for anything new? No, no new problems. My heart is good, my blood pressure is fine—I check it at the pharmacy sometimes when I go to pick up my sister's old friend's birthday cards, they have one of those machines you stick your arm in, you know? And medications, no, I haven't started anything new at all. I just take my daily vitamin, the ones with the extra calcium because women my age need to watch their bones, and sometimes a little aspirin if my knees are really acting up. But no new prescriptions or anything Dr. Henderson has given me. Just the same old routine. I try to keep things simple, you know? I don't like taking too many pills if I don't have to. My friend Martha, she takes about fifteen different pills a day, and she's always getting them mixed up, taking her morning pill at night and her night pill in the morning... I just wouldn't want to deal with all that confusion. So, no, nothing new in the medicine cabinet and no new ailments, just these strange freezing spells with my hands and my arms.\n\nDoctor: The next time you find yourself stuck like that, could you try to see if your arm stays exactly where you put it if someone were to gently move it for you, and then tell me if it felt like it was resisting you or if it just moved like a soft, flexible object?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I could try to do that, Doctor. I really could. The thing is, it's just so hard to know when it's going to happen next, you see? It's not like I wake up in the morning and think, \"Oh, today's the day I'm going to get stuck holding the milk jug.\" It just sneaks up on me. Like that time at the church dinner I was telling you about, or the time I was trying to hang my good blue cardigan on the clothesline—the one with the pearl buttons my sister gave me for my sixtieth birthday—and I just froze right there with the clothespin in my hand. He's such a serious man, always talking about the book of Leviticus and looking so stern. I think he'd be terribly uncomfortable. \n\nBut I understand why you're asking, Doctor. They don't know what to do with their hands or where to look. But... I'll try to keep it in mind. I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm not running any foot races these days, being seventy-one and all, but I get around just as well as I did five, maybe even ten years ago. I walk down to the mailbox every single morning right at ten o'clock when Mr. Peterson delivers the mail—he's been our mailman for... oh, it must be fifteen years now, such a nice man, always waves at me. It makes me feel so bad for her, it really does. She says it's just her nerves, but I don't know. \n\nBut me? No, my hands are steady as a rock. I can still thread a needle on the first try most days, well, as long as I have my good bifocals on and the light from the window is hitting it just right. I was just hemming a pair of slacks last week, the brown ones I like to wear to the grocery store because they don't show the dirt if I brush up against the car, and my hands didn't shake one bit. So when I'm just sitting in my parlor watching my afternoon programs, my hands are perfectly still. Just resting there on the armrests, quiet as can be. It's really just that strange freezing up business with my arms that's the trouble. No shaking, and no slowing down. Just steady, regular old me.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, are you still fully aware of everything happening around you, or does it feel like you've drifted off and aren't really there anymore?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I haven't drifted off anywhere. I am fully aware. Yes, indeed. I haven't gone daydreaming or fallen asleep standing up, if that's what you mean. I am right there in the room, wide awake, seeing and hearing every little thing. And I'll tell you, Doctor, that is exactly the hardest part of it all, being so aware. In fact, to be perfectly honest with you, that is the whole reason I finally made the appointment to come see you in the first place. I came because I keep doing these terribly odd things, especially over at the church, and I know I'm doing them while it's happening! \n\nIt’s just so embarrassing. Like at the fellowship hall for our Sunday potluck—we always have a big spread on the first Sunday of the month, and Mary brings her green bean casserole with those little crispy onions on top, which everyone just loves. Well, I was standing right by the potluck table, right at the front of the line where everyone is holding their paper plates and getting hungry, and I just got stuck. I was holding the big plastic serving spoon straight up in the air, and I just wasn't moving. I was frozen there like a statue with that spoon, looking down at the macaroni salad, and I could hear everyone behind me. I could hear Mr. Davis clearing his throat—he always gets so impatient when it comes to his food—and I knew they were all staring, but I just couldn't bring my arm down. \n\nAnd it’s not just the freezing up like that, either. Sometimes I get stuck doing something over and over, but I'm fully awake for that, too. Like with the napkins. Just the other week, I was supposed to be setting out the paper napkins for the dessert table—we had a lovely lemon pound cake that afternoon—and I just stood there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. I'd fold them in half, then unfold them, then fold them again, smoothing down the edges with my thumb, just doing it again and again. And the whole time, there were people waiting on me to finish so they could get their cake and their coffee! I could see them out of the corner of my eye, whispering to each other, probably wondering if poor Mildred had finally lost her marbles, but I just kept folding that same little stack. \n\nSo, you see, I haven't drifted off to some other place at all. I know exactly what's going on around me. I know I'm doing these odd things, and I know people are waiting on me or staring at me, but I just get stuck doing them anyway. It’s the strangest, most frustrating feeling in the world, knowing you're making a spectacle of yourself but not being able to just stop and act normal.\n\nDoctor: If someone were to gently move your arm while you are frozen in one of these spells, would it stay exactly where they put it, like a mannequin's arm, or would it naturally spring back to the position you were holding?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know the answer to that. They just stare and whisper to each other behind their hands. Her denial of hallucinations, delusions, or medication changes keeps F06.1 (organic catatonia), F20.2 (catatonic schizophrenia), and F44.2 (dissociative stupor) as the primary differentials. You need to know if it feels stiff or if it's... what did you say? Like a soft, flexible object. Like a ragdoll, maybe. I just don't know how I'd manage to get someone to test it for me without causing a whole commotion. People get so flustered when things aren't normal. They don't know what to do with their hands or where to look. But... I'll try to keep it in mind. If I'm with someone, maybe my friend Martha—though she's so forgetful she'd probably think I was just playing a game with her—I'll try to see if they can move me, and I'll pay real close attention to how it feels. I'll do my best to remember, I really will, but it's just such a strange thing to plan for, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you are walking or going about your day, have you noticed that your movements have become slower, or that you have developed any shaking in your hands when they are just resting at your sides?\n\nPatient: Well... slower? I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm not running any foot races these days, being seventy-one and all, but I get around just as well as I did five, maybe even ten years ago. I walk down to the mailbox every single morning right at ten o'clock when Mr. Peterson delivers the mail—he's been our mailman for... oh, it must be fifteen years now, such a nice man, always waves at me. And I still do all my own vacuuming, too. Pushing that heavy old Hoover around the living room rug is quite the chore, especially getting around the edges of the coffee table, but I get it done in the exact same amount of time I always have. I haven't slowed down a bit, really, unless you count being careful on the porch steps when they're wet, but that's just plain common sense, isn't it? You don't want to take a tumble at my age. \n\nAnd as for shaking... no, no, my hands don't shake at all. Not when I'm just sitting there, and not when I'm doing things, either. Now, my friend Martha, the one I was telling you about with all the pills? Her hands shake terribly. We were sitting out on her patio just last Sunday having a nice glass of sweet tea, and the ice cubes in her glass were just clinking and clattering the whole time because her hands tremble so much, even when they're just resting right there in her lap. It makes me feel so bad for her, it really does. She says it's just her nerves, but I don't know. \n\nBut me? No, my hands are steady as a rock. I can still thread a needle on the first try most days, well, as long as I have my good bifocals on and the light from the window is hitting it just right. I was just hemming a pair of slacks last week, the brown ones I like to wear to the grocery store because they don't show the dirt if I brush up against the car, and my hands didn't shake one bit. So when I'm just sitting in my parlor watching my afternoon programs, my hands are perfectly still. Just resting there on the armrests, quiet as can be. It's really just that strange freezing up business with my arms that's the trouble. No shaking, and no slowing down. Just steady, regular old me.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, are you still fully aware of everything happening around you, or does it feel like you've drifted off and aren't really there anymore?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I haven't drifted off anywhere. I am fully aware. Yes, indeed. I haven't gone daydreaming or fallen asleep standing up, if that's what you mean. I am right there in the room, wide awake, seeing and hearing every little thing. And I'll tell you, Doctor, that is exactly the hardest part of it all, being so aware. In fact, to be perfectly honest with you, that is the whole reason I finally made the appointment to come see you in the first place. I came because I keep doing these terribly odd things, especially over at the church, and I know I'm doing them while it's happening! \n\nIt’s just so embarrassing. Like at the fellowship hall for our Sunday potluck—we always have a big spread on the first Sunday of the month, and Mary brings her green bean casserole with those little crispy onions on top, which everyone just loves. Well, I was standing right by the potluck table, right at the front of the line where everyone is holding their paper plates and getting hungry, and I just got stuck. I was holding the big plastic serving spoon straight up in the air, and I just wasn't moving. I was frozen there like a statue with that spoon, looking down at the macaroni salad, and I could hear everyone behind me. I could hear Mr. Davis clearing his throat—he always gets so impatient when it comes to his food—and I knew they were all staring, but I just couldn't bring my arm down. \n\nAnd it’s not just the freezing up like that, either. Sometimes I get stuck doing something over and over, but I'm fully awake for that, too. Like with the napkins. Just the other week, I was supposed to be setting out the paper napkins for the dessert table—we had a lovely lemon pound cake that afternoon—and I just stood there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. I'd fold them in half, then unfold them, then fold them again, smoothing down the edges with my thumb, just doing it again and again. And the whole time, there were people waiting on me to finish so they could get their cake and their coffee! I could see them out of the corner of my eye, whispering to each other, probably wondering if poor Mildred had finally lost her marbles, but I just kept folding that same little stack. \n\nSo, you see, I haven't drifted off to some other place at all. I know exactly what's going on around me. I know I'm doing these odd things, and I know people are waiting on me or staring at me, but I just get stuck doing them anyway. It’s the strangest, most frustrating feeling in the world, knowing you're making a spectacle of yourself but not being able to just stop and act normal.\n\nDoctor: If someone were to gently move your arm while you are frozen in one of these spells, would it stay exactly where they put it, like a mannequin's arm, or would it naturally spring back to the position you were holding?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know the answer to that. Like I was saying a few minutes ago about trying to get someone to test it for me... nobody has ever actually come up and touched my arm when I’m stuck in one of those spells. \n\nPeople are so funny about personal space, aren't they? It's not like the old days where a neighbor would just step right in to help. When I was frozen there with that big plastic serving spoon at the church potluck, Mr. Davis was standing right behind me—close enough that I could smell the peppermint he always keeps in his shirt pocket—but did he reach out and try to pull my arm down or take the spoon from me? No, sir, he certainly did not. He just stood there clearing his throat and making a fuss. \n\nSo I just don't have the faintest idea if my arm would stay put like one of those mannequins in the department store windows—you know, like the ones they used to have at the Sears before it closed down out on the highway, the ones with the painted-on eyelashes—or if it would just snap right back into place like an old rubber band. Since I live all by myself in that big house, just me and my knickknacks, there's nobody around to try moving me when it happens at home. And when I'm out and about, well, folks just stare. They just stare and whisper to each other behind their hands. They don't want to get involved, I suppose, or maybe they're afraid they'll hurt me. \n\nSo unless somebody gets brave and decides to grab hold of my arm the next time I turn into a statue, I really couldn't say what would happen. I wish I could be more helpful to you, Doctor, I really do, because I want to get to the bottom of this just as much as you do, but I just haven't had anyone try it yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's... that's a bit hard to say, too, to be perfectly honest with you. I don't really know if they feel rigid like they're locked up, or floppy like a ragdoll. Because, well, nobody has ever really "broken the spell" for me, either. I just sort of... come out of it on my own, eventually. 

Like at the church, with the serving spoon and the macaroni salad? Nobody touched me or shook me or yelled my name to snap me out of it. I just stood there for what felt like an eternity—though it was probably only a minute or two, knowing how time plays tricks on you when you're embarrassed—and then, suddenly, my arm just started moving again. I just scooped the salad onto my plate and kept walking down the line, trying to pretend my face wasn't as red as a ripe tomato. 

And when I come out of it, my muscles just feel... normal, I suppose? They don't ache like I've been clenching them tight, like when you carry too many grocery bags in from the car at once and your shoulders get all knotted up. But they don't feel weak or floppy, either. I don't collapse or drop what I'm holding. I just go right back to whatever I was doing before I got stuck. 

So I really couldn't tell you if they're tense or relaxed while it's happening, because I'm so caught up in the embarrassment of it all, and nobody has ever grabbed me to see. It's just... I'm stuck, and then I'm not. And then I have to face everyone who was watching me, which is a whole other kind of stiffness, let me tell you.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.1, F20.2, F44.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's nice to meet you; could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Dr. Smith. Well, it's nice to meet you too, I suppose. I... well, I came in today because things have just been getting so strange lately, mostly over at the church. I go every Sunday, have for forty years, and I always help out with the fellowship meals. I like to be helpful, you know, since I don't work and never had much schooling, it's my way of doing my part. \n\nBut lately... I just don't know what comes over me. I keep doing these odd things, just real strange things. Like, just the other week, I was standing by the potluck table, right? They had me on the hot dishes. And I picked up the serving spoon for the green beans, and I just held it right up in the air and... I just stopped. I didn't move at all. I was just standing there like a statue with the spoon up, and the whole line of folks was just staring at me with their empty plates, wondering what on earth I was doing. \n\nAnd it's not just that one time, either. Another time, I was in charge of the paper goods, and I just kept folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. Just creasing them, smoothing them out, folding them back up, over and over, while folks were waiting on me to hand them out. Poor Mrs. Gable was just standing right there waiting for a napkin for her plate and I couldn't seem to stop messing with that same little stack. It's just so embarrassing, Doctor, people waiting on me and me acting all mixed up like that, and I just don't understand why my hands and my body are doing it...\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself folding those napkins over and over, do you feel an internal pressure or a need to do it to prevent something bad from happening or to stop yourself from feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that at all. It's not like I'm worried the church roof is going to cave in or somebody's going to catch a cold if I don't fold them just right. I know some folks get nervous like that, but for me... I don't feel anxious or scared or anything. Actually, I don't feel much of anything at all when it happens. \n\nIt's just... well, my hands just keep doing it on their own. I'll be looking at the napkins—they were those nice thick yellow ones last week, you know, because Pastor Thomas always likes the bright colors when spring is coming around, he says it lifts the spirit—and my hands are just creasing the edges. I don't have a thought in my head telling me I have to do it, or that I'm trying to fix anything or stop anything bad. It's more like my brain just sort of... empties out, or gets stuck on a loop. And I'm just watching my own hands fold and smooth, fold and smooth, and I'm not scared, I'm just doing it. \n\nUntil suddenly I realize Mrs. Gable is clearing her throat, and she's got that tight little look around her mouth she gets when she's impatient, and then I feel embarrassed. But while I'm doing it? No, no pressure inside me, no bad feelings I'm trying to chase away. Just... folding. It's the strangest thing, Doctor, because if I was doing it for a reason, even a foolish reason, I think I could understand it better, but there just isn't any reason at all.\n\nDoctor: When you are stuck in those positions, like holding the spoon in the air, if I were to gently try to move your arm, would it feel like it's stuck in place, or would it move easily like a mannequin's arm?\n\nPatient: Well, that's a good question, Doctor. You know, I've thought about that myself, wondering what it would feel like if someone just reached out and tried to pull my arm down when I was holding that spoon. Because, you see, nobody actually did try to move me. They just stood there, staring. I think they were too surprised or maybe a little frightened to touch me, which I understand. If I saw someone standing there like a statue with a spoonful of green beans, I might just stare too! I mean, you don't want to upset someone who's acting peculiar, especially at a church dinner where everyone's trying to keep things polite and nice.\n\nBut as for how it would feel if you did try to move my arm... I honestly just don't know. I can't really say if it would be stiff like a board or if it would just bend easy like one of those department store mannequins. It's hard to explain, but when I'm in that state, I'm not really paying attention to my muscles or how tight they are. I'm just... there. Stuck. So since nobody has ever actually taken hold of my arm or tried to push it down or move it out of the way, I really couldn't tell you what it would feel like for them or for me. I just know that I don't move it myself until the spell, or whatever you want to call it, finally breaks.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these physical episodes, have you ever heard voices that others couldn't hear, or seen things that weren't really there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, doctor. No, nothing like that. My goodness, if I started hearing voices on top of the spoon business, I just don't know what I'd do. I live all by myself, you see, ever since my sister passed away—oh, it's been about six years now, bless her heart. So my little house is very, very quiet. Sometimes a bit too quiet, if I'm being perfectly honest with you. The only voices I hear are when I turn on my television programs in the afternoon, or... well, sometimes if Mrs. Higgins next door is fussing at her little dog, Buster. He's a sweet little terrier mix but he just loves to dig up her petunias, so she's always hollering out the back door at him. But inside my own head? Or in an empty room? No, never. \n\nI mean, my hearing isn't quite what it used to be, what with me being seventy-one now, but I definitely don't hear people talking who aren't really there. And as for seeing things... well, my eyesight is getting a little fuzzy around the edges. I actually had to get new bifocals just last year because I couldn't even read the hymnal at church anymore without squinting terribly, and Pastor Thomas prints them in a nice big font, too. But I only see what's really right in front of me. Like the napkins, or the hot dishes. I've never seen a ghost or a vision or anything out of the ordinary like that. Just the regular old world. It's really just my body doing these strange freezing up things... my mind itself is as quiet as can be. Just me and the ticking clock in the parlor most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any other new physical health problems, or have you started taking any new medications recently?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. My health has been pretty steady, thank the Lord. I mean, I have my usual aches and pains, of course. My knees get a little stiff when it rains—we had that terrible downpour last Tuesday, and I could hardly get up the porch steps to get the mail, but that's just getting older, isn't it? Dr. Henderson, my regular doctor, he always says, \"Mildred, at seventy-one, you're doing better than most,\" which is nice of him to say, even if he does always keep me waiting in his office for nearly an hour. \n\nBut as for anything new? No, no new problems. My heart is good, my blood pressure is fine—I check it at the pharmacy sometimes when I go to pick up my sister's old friend's birthday cards, they have one of those machines you stick your arm in, you know? And medications, no, I haven't started anything new at all. I just take my daily vitamin, the ones with the extra calcium because women my age need to watch their bones, and sometimes a little aspirin if my knees are really acting up. But no new prescriptions or anything Dr. Henderson has given me. Just the same old routine. I try to keep things simple, you know? I don't like taking too many pills if I don't have to. My friend Martha, she takes about fifteen different pills a day, and she's always getting them mixed up, taking her morning pill at night and her night pill in the morning... I just wouldn't want to deal with all that confusion. So, no, nothing new in the medicine cabinet and no new ailments, just these strange freezing spells with my hands and my arms.\n\nDoctor: The next time you find yourself stuck like that, could you try to see if your arm stays exactly where you put it if someone were to gently move it for you, and then tell me if it felt like it was resisting you or if it just moved like a soft, flexible object?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I could try to do that, Doctor. I really could. The thing is, it's just so hard to know when it's going to happen next, you see? It's not like I wake up in the morning and think, \"Oh, today's the day I'm going to get stuck holding the milk jug.\" It just sneaks up on me. Like that time at the church dinner I was telling you about, or the time I was trying to hang my good blue cardigan on the clothesline—the one with the pearl buttons my sister gave me for my sixtieth birthday—and I just froze right there with the clothespin in my hand. Mrs. Higgins was out in her yard, too, and she must have thought I was out of my mind, just standing there with my arm up in the air like I was waving to a bird or something. \n\nBut if it does happen again, and if there's someone around—which is a big \"if,\" considering I live alone and I'm only really around people on Sundays or when I go to the Piggly Wiggly on Thursday mornings—I suppose I could ask them to try and move my arm. Or, well, I guess I wouldn't be able to ask them right then and there, because I can't really talk when I'm stuck like that. I'd have to explain it to them beforehand, wouldn't I? I'd have to say, \"Now, if you see me freeze up like a statue, just come over and give my arm a little push and see what happens.\" Oh my, can you imagine the look on Pastor Thomas's face if I asked him to do that? He's such a serious man, always talking about the book of Leviticus and looking so stern. I think he'd be terribly uncomfortable. \n\nBut I understand why you're asking, Doctor. You need to know if it feels stiff or if it's... what did you say? Like a soft, flexible object. Like a ragdoll, maybe. I just don't know how I'd manage to get someone to test it for me without causing a whole commotion. People get so flustered when things aren't normal. They don't know what to do with their hands or where to look. But... I'll try to keep it in mind. If I'm with someone, maybe my friend Martha—though she's so forgetful she'd probably think I was just playing a game with her—I'll try to see if they can move me, and I'll pay real close attention to how it feels. I'll do my best to remember, I really will, but it's just such a strange thing to plan for, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you are walking or going about your day, have you noticed that your movements have become slower, or that you have developed any shaking in your hands when they are just resting at your sides?\n\nPatient: Well... slower? I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm not running any foot races these days, being seventy-one and all, but I get around just as well as I did five, maybe even ten years ago. I walk down to the mailbox every single morning right at ten o'clock when Mr. Peterson delivers the mail—he's been our mailman for... oh, it must be fifteen years now, such a nice man, always waves at me. And I still do all my own vacuuming, too. Pushing that heavy old Hoover around the living room rug is quite the chore, especially getting around the edges of the coffee table, but I get it done in the exact same amount of time I always have. I haven't slowed down a bit, really, unless you count being careful on the porch steps when they're wet, but that's just plain common sense, isn't it? You don't want to take a tumble at my age. \n\nAnd as for shaking... no, no, my hands don't shake at all. Not when I'm just sitting there, and not when I'm doing things, either. Now, my friend Martha, the one I was telling you about with all the pills? Her hands shake terribly. We were sitting out on her patio just last Sunday having a nice glass of sweet tea, and the ice cubes in her glass were just clinking and clattering the whole time because her hands tremble so much, even when they're just resting right there in her lap. It makes me feel so bad for her, it really does. She says it's just her nerves, but I don't know. \n\nBut me? No, my hands are steady as a rock. I can still thread a needle on the first try most days, well, as long as I have my good bifocals on and the light from the window is hitting it just right. I was just hemming a pair of slacks last week, the brown ones I like to wear to the grocery store because they don't show the dirt if I brush up against the car, and my hands didn't shake one bit. So when I'm just sitting in my parlor watching my afternoon programs, my hands are perfectly still. Just resting there on the armrests, quiet as can be. It's really just that strange freezing up business with my arms that's the trouble. No shaking, and no slowing down. Just steady, regular old me.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, are you still fully aware of everything happening around you, or does it feel like you've drifted off and aren't really there anymore?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I haven't drifted off anywhere. I am fully aware. Yes, indeed. I haven't gone daydreaming or fallen asleep standing up, if that's what you mean. I am right there in the room, wide awake, seeing and hearing every little thing. And I'll tell you, Doctor, that is exactly the hardest part of it all, being so aware. In fact, to be perfectly honest with you, that is the whole reason I finally made the appointment to come see you in the first place. I came because I keep doing these terribly odd things, especially over at the church, and I know I'm doing them while it's happening! \n\nIt’s just so embarrassing. Like at the fellowship hall for our Sunday potluck—we always have a big spread on the first Sunday of the month, and Mary brings her green bean casserole with those little crispy onions on top, which everyone just loves. Well, I was standing right by the potluck table, right at the front of the line where everyone is holding their paper plates and getting hungry, and I just got stuck. I was holding the big plastic serving spoon straight up in the air, and I just wasn't moving. I was frozen there like a statue with that spoon, looking down at the macaroni salad, and I could hear everyone behind me. I could hear Mr. Davis clearing his throat—he always gets so impatient when it comes to his food—and I knew they were all staring, but I just couldn't bring my arm down. \n\nAnd it’s not just the freezing up like that, either. Sometimes I get stuck doing something over and over, but I'm fully awake for that, too. Like with the napkins. Just the other week, I was supposed to be setting out the paper napkins for the dessert table—we had a lovely lemon pound cake that afternoon—and I just stood there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. I'd fold them in half, then unfold them, then fold them again, smoothing down the edges with my thumb, just doing it again and again. And the whole time, there were people waiting on me to finish so they could get their cake and their coffee! I could see them out of the corner of my eye, whispering to each other, probably wondering if poor Mildred had finally lost her marbles, but I just kept folding that same little stack. \n\nSo, you see, I haven't drifted off to some other place at all. I know exactly what's going on around me. I know I'm doing these odd things, and I know people are waiting on me or staring at me, but I just get stuck doing them anyway. It’s the strangest, most frustrating feeling in the world, knowing you're making a spectacle of yourself but not being able to just stop and act normal.\n\nDoctor: If someone were to gently move your arm while you are frozen in one of these spells, would it stay exactly where they put it, like a mannequin's arm, or would it naturally spring back to the position you were holding?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know the answer to that. Like I was saying a few minutes ago about trying to get someone to test it for me... nobody has ever actually come up and touched my arm when I’m stuck in one of those spells. \n\nPeople are so funny about personal space, aren't they? It's not like the old days where a neighbor would just step right in to help. When I was frozen there with that big plastic serving spoon at the church potluck, Mr. Davis was standing right behind me—close enough that I could smell the peppermint he always keeps in his shirt pocket—but did he reach out and try to pull my arm down or take the spoon from me? No, sir, he certainly did not. He just stood there clearing his throat and making a fuss. \n\nSo I just don't have the faintest idea if my arm would stay put like one of those mannequins in the department store windows—you know, like the ones they used to have at the Sears before it closed down out on the highway, the ones with the painted-on eyelashes—or if it would just snap right back into place like an old rubber band. Since I live all by myself in that big house, just me and my knickknacks, there's nobody around to try moving me when it happens at home. And when I'm out and about, well, folks just stare. They just stare and whisper to each other behind their hands. They don't want to get involved, I suppose, or maybe they're afraid they'll hurt me. \n\nSo unless somebody gets brave and decides to grab hold of my arm the next time I turn into a statue, I really couldn't say what would happen. I wish I could be more helpful to you, Doctor, I really do, because I want to get to the bottom of this just as much as you do, but I just haven't had anyone try it yet.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these states and someone finally does break the spell, do you find that your muscles feel very tense and rigid, like they were locked in place, or do they feel completely relaxed and floppy, like you have no strength in them at all?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a bit hard to say, too, to be perfectly honest with you. I don't really know if they feel rigid like they're locked up, or floppy like a ragdoll. Because, well, nobody has ever really \"broken the spell\" for me, either. I just sort of... come out of it on my own, eventually. \n\nLike at the church, with the serving spoon and the macaroni salad? Nobody touched me or shook me or yelled my name to snap me out of it. I just stood there for what felt like an eternity—though it was probably only a minute or two, knowing how time plays tricks on you when you're embarrassed—and then, suddenly, my arm just started moving again. I just scooped the salad onto my plate and kept walking down the line, trying to pretend my face wasn't as red as a ripe tomato. \n\nAnd when I come out of it, my muscles just feel... normal, I suppose? They don't ache like I've been clenching them tight, like when you carry too many grocery bags in from the car at once and your shoulders get all knotted up. But they don't feel weak or floppy, either. I don't collapse or drop what I'm holding. I just go right back to whatever I was doing before I got stuck. \n\nSo I really couldn't tell you if they're tense or relaxed while it's happening, because I'm so caught up in the embarrassment of it all, and nobody has ever grabbed me to see. It's just... I'm stuck, and then I'm not. And then I have to face everyone who was watching me, which is a whole other kind of stiffness, let me tell you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

  "note": "The patient's clear description of being fully awake and aware during episodes of 'freezing' or repetitive behavior, coupled with the absence of negative symptoms, delusions, or hallucinations, makes catatonia associated with schizophrenia (F20.2) or organic brain disease (F06.1) significantly less likely. The presentation is highly consistent with dissociative stupor, which is classified under F44.2.",

So, no, nothing new in the medicine cabinet and no new ailments, just these strange freezing spells with my hands and my arms.\n\nDoctor: The next time you find yourself stuck like that, could you try to see if your arm stays exactly where you put it if someone were to gently move it for you, and then tell me if it felt like it was resisting you or if it just moved like a soft, flexible object?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose I could try to do that, Doctor. I really could. The thing is, it's just so hard to know when it's going to happen next, you see? It's not like I wake up in the morning and think, \"Oh, today's the day I'm going to get stuck holding the milk jug.\" It just sneaks up on me. Like that time at the church dinner I was telling you about, or the time I was trying to hang my good blue cardigan on the clothesline—the one with the pearl buttons my sister gave me for my sixtieth birthday—and I just froze right there with the clothespin in my hand. Mrs. Higgins was out in her yard, too, and she must have thought I was out of my mind, just standing there with my arm up in the air like I was waving to a bird or something. \n\nBut if it does happen again, and if there's someone around—which is a big \"if,\" considering I live alone and I'm only really around people on Sundays or when I go to the Piggly Wiggly on Thursday mornings—I suppose I could ask them to try and move my arm. Or, well, I guess I wouldn't be able to ask them right then and there, because I can't really talk when I'm stuck like that. I'd have to explain it to them beforehand, wouldn't I? I'd have to say, \"Now, if you see me freeze up like a statue, just come over and give my arm a little push and see what happens.\" Oh my, can you imagine the look on Pastor Thomas's face if I asked him to do that? He's such a serious man, always talking about the book of Leviticus and looking so stern. I think he'd be terribly uncomfortable. \n\nBut I understand why you're asking, Doctor. You need to know if it feels stiff or if it's... what did you say? Like a soft, flexible object. Like a ragdoll, maybe. I just don't know how I'd manage to get someone to test it for me without causing a whole commotion. People get so flustered when things aren't normal. They don't know what to do with their hands or where to look. But... I'll try to keep it in mind. If I'm with someone, maybe my friend Martha—though she's so forgetful she'd probably think I was just playing a game with her—I'll try to see if they can move me, and I'll pay real close attention to how it feels. I'll do my best to remember, I really will, but it's just such a strange thing to plan for, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: When you are walking or going about your day, have you noticed that your movements have become slower, or that you have developed any shaking in your hands when they are just resting at your sides?\n\nPatient: Well... slower? I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm not running any foot races these days, being seventy-one and all, but I get around just as well as I did five, maybe even ten years ago. I walk down to the mailbox every single morning right at ten o'clock when Mr. Peterson delivers the mail—he's been our mailman for... oh, it must be fifteen years now, such a nice man, always waves at me. And I still do all my own vacuuming, too. Pushing that heavy old Hoover around the living room rug is quite the chore, especially getting around the edges of the coffee table, but I get it done in the exact same amount of time I always have. I haven't slowed down a bit, really, unless you count being careful on the porch steps when they're wet, but that's just plain common sense, isn't it? You don't want to take a tumble at my age. \n\nAnd as for shaking... no, no, my hands don't shake at all. Not when I'm just sitting there, and not when I'm doing things, either. Now, my friend Martha, the one I was telling you about with all the pills? Her hands shake terribly. We were sitting out on her patio just last Sunday having a nice glass of sweet tea, and the ice cubes in her glass were just clinking and clattering the whole time because her hands tremble so much, even when they're just resting right there in her lap. It makes me feel so bad for her, it really does. She says it's just her nerves, but I don't know. \n\nBut me? No, my hands are steady as a rock. I can still thread a needle on the first try most days, well, as long as I have my good bifocals on and the light from the window is hitting it just right. I was just hemming a pair of slacks last week, the brown ones I like to wear to the grocery store because they don't show the dirt if I brush up against the car, and my hands didn't shake one bit. So when I'm just sitting in my parlor watching my afternoon programs, my hands are perfectly still. Just resting there on the armrests, quiet as can be. It's really just that strange freezing up business with my arms that's the trouble. No shaking, and no slowing down. Just steady, regular old me.\n\nDoctor: When you are frozen like that, are you still fully aware of everything happening around you, or does it feel like you've drifted off and aren't really there anymore?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I haven't drifted off anywhere. I am fully aware. Yes, indeed. I haven't gone daydreaming or fallen asleep standing up, if that's what you mean. I am right there in the room, wide awake, seeing and hearing every little thing. And I'll tell you, Doctor, that is exactly the hardest part of it all, being so aware. In fact, to be perfectly honest with you, that is the whole reason I finally made the appointment to come see you in the first place. I came because I keep doing these terribly odd things, especially over at the church, and I know I'm doing them while it's happening! \n\nIt’s just so embarrassing. Like at the fellowship hall for our Sunday potluck—we always have a big spread on the first Sunday of the month, and Mary brings her green bean casserole with those little crispy onions on top, which everyone just loves. Well, I was standing right by the potluck table, right at the front of the line where everyone is holding their paper plates and getting hungry, and I just got stuck. I was holding the big plastic serving spoon straight up in the air, and I just wasn't moving. I was frozen there like a statue with that spoon, looking down at the macaroni salad, and I could hear everyone behind me. I could hear Mr. Davis clearing his throat—he always gets so impatient when it comes to his food—and I knew they were all staring, but I just couldn't bring my arm down. \n\nAnd it’s not just the freezing up like that, either. Sometimes I get stuck doing something over and over, but I'm fully awake for that, too. Like with the napkins. Just the other week, I was supposed to be setting out the paper napkins for the dessert table—we had a lovely lemon pound cake that afternoon—and I just stood there folding the exact same stack of napkins over and over again. I'd fold them in half, then unfold them, then fold them again, smoothing down the edges with my thumb, just doing it again and again. And the whole time, there were people waiting on me to finish so they could get their cake and their coffee! I could see them out of the corner of my eye, whispering to each other, probably wondering if poor Mildred had finally lost her marbles, but I just kept folding that same little stack. \n\nSo, you see, I haven't drifted off to some other place at all. I know exactly what's going on around me. I know I'm doing these odd things, and I know people are waiting on me or staring at me, but I just get stuck doing them anyway. It’s the strangest, most frustrating feeling in the world, knowing you're making a spectacle of yourself but not being able to just stop and act normal.\n\nDoctor: If someone were to gently move your arm while you are frozen in one of these spells, would it stay exactly where they put it, like a mannequin's arm, or would it naturally spring back to the position you were holding?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, I... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know the answer to that. Like I was saying a few minutes ago about trying to get someone to test it for me... nobody has ever actually come up and touched my arm when I’m stuck in one of those spells. \n\nPeople are so funny about personal space, aren't they? It's not like the old days where a neighbor would just step right in to help. When I was frozen there with that big plastic serving spoon at the church potluck, Mr. Davis was standing right behind me—close enough that I could smell the peppermint he always keeps in his shirt pocket—but did he reach out and try to pull my arm down or take the spoon from me? No, sir, he certainly did not. He just stood there clearing his throat and making a fuss. \n\nSo I just don't have the faintest idea if my arm would stay put like one of those mannequins in the department store windows—you know, like the ones they used to have at the Sears before it closed down out on the highway, the ones with the painted-on eyelashes—or if it would just snap right back into place like an old rubber band. Since I live all by myself in that big house, just me and my knickknacks, there's nobody around to try moving me when it happens at home. And when I'm out and about, well, folks just stare. They just stare and whisper to each other behind their hands. They don't want to get involved, I suppose, or maybe they're afraid they'll hurt me. \n\nSo unless somebody gets brave and decides to grab hold of my arm the next time I turn into a statue, I really couldn't say what would happen. I wish I could be more helpful to you, Doctor, I really do, because I want to get to the bottom of this just as much as you do, but I just haven't had anyone try it yet.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these states and someone finally does break the spell, do you find that your muscles feel very tense and rigid, like they were locked in place, or do they feel completely relaxed and floppy, like you have no strength in them at all?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... that's a bit hard to say, too, to be perfectly honest with you. I don't really know if they feel rigid like they're locked up, or floppy like a ragdoll. Because, well, nobody has ever really \"broken the spell\" for me, either. I just sort of... come out of it on my own, eventually. \n\nLike at the church, with the serving spoon and the macaroni salad? Nobody touched me or shook me or yelled my name to snap me out of it. I just stood there for what felt like an eternity—though it was probably only a minute or two, knowing how time plays tricks on you when you're embarrassed—and then, suddenly, my arm just started moving again. I just scooped the salad onto my plate and kept walking down the line, trying to pretend my face wasn't as red as a ripe tomato. \n\nAnd when I come out of it, my muscles just feel... normal, I suppose? They don't ache like I've been clenching them tight, like when you carry too many grocery bags in from the car at once and your shoulders get all knotted up. But they don't feel weak or floppy, either. I don't collapse or drop what I'm holding. I just go right back to whatever I was doing before I got stuck. \n\nSo I really couldn't tell you if they're tense or relaxed while it's happening, because I'm so caught up in the embarrassment of it all, and nobody has ever grabbed me to see. It's just... I'm stuck, and then I'm not. And then I have to face everyone who was watching me, which is a whole other kind of stiffness, let me tell you."
  }
